1235. DANIEL LAMBERT was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 1s., the goods of Herbert Mayo, from his person.
HERBERT MAYO. On the 28th of April, about two o'clock, I was in Newgate-street; I felt my coat-pocket had been picked, and missed my handkerchief - I turned, and saw several persons; the prisoner was within one or two of me - I thought he appeared the likeliest person to have it; I watched him - he crossed the way, and then began to run; I followed, and caused him to be apprehended - I did not lose sight of him; when I came up to him my handkerchief lay at his feet - he had run about one hundred yards; I did not see where the handkerchief came from - he was about fifteen yards before me when the officer stopped him; none of the persons I saw near me had run with him.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. How many persons were within five yards of you? A. Eight or ten; I cannot say - he was the nearest; he began to run alone - I kept my eyes on him; I did not see the handkerchief drop from him - he did not walk some yards before he crossed the road.
JOSEPH BATES. I am an officer. I saw the prisoner running, and heard Stop thief! called - I saw him take this handkerchief from his bosom with his left hand, and throw it over his shoulder; I was close to him - no persons were so near as Mr. Mayo and myself.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you a regular officer? A. Yes; I belong to Farringdon-ward; the prisoner was ten or fifteen yards before me when I heard the alarm -I was sitting at my dinner at the corner of Warwick-square; he tried to knock one or two young men down; there was not a soul to be seen, but the mob who were following him - I took him in Warwick-lane, near Amencorner.
MR. MAYO. I believe this to be my handkerchief - it is the same pattern.
Cross-examined. Q. Is it not a common pattern? A. Yes; but I believe it to be mine; there is no difference between this and the one I lost.
GUILTY. Aged 16.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18300527-206]
1236. WILLIAM HAMPTON was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of May, 1480 sheets of printed paper, value 26s., the goods of George Eyre and others.
RICHARD PARADISE. I am in the employ of George Eyre and Andrew Strachan, printer s. The prisoner came there to get some sheets for the binders - he came two or three times; on Saturday, the 15th of May, he brought a note from the bookbinders between three and four o'clock in the afternoon, and brought back a sheet which he had had the day before - he turned to go out, and said he would call again; as he was going out I heard a rustling at the warehouse door - I followed, and found this bundle of paper on his shoulder: I had seen it in the office that day - he had not got above twelve yards from our warehouse - our door opens into the passage; I made him bring it back - he said he had picked it up in the passage as he was going out; this is it - it is what we call waste: it is worth 1l. 6s.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did not he say he was bringing it back? A. No - I tapped him on the shoulder, and told him to bring it back; I had been in the room where the paper was, but did not see it taken - there was only another person in that office.
EDWARD WYNNE. I am foreman to Messrs. Eyre and Strachan; Mr. Spottiswoode superintends the firm, as Mr. Eyre resides in the country, and Mr. Strachan is infirm -I left Paradise in care of the place in my absence - on my return I found the prisoner in custody.
Cross-examined. Q. Is Mr. Eyre alive? A. He was a few days ago; I believe Mr. Spottiswoode is a partner -I never saw his name to any document; I have seen the names of George Eyre and Andrew Strachan - I do not know whether Mr. Spottiswoode signs cheques or not; I am not in the counting-house.
JOHN JAMES BERGE (one of the Jury.) I am a stationer, and deal with the prosecutors. We understand Mr. Spottiswoode is a partner, but I do not know that his name appears - I cannot say that he has ever signed his name.
Cross-examined. Q. Do not you know that in the Acts of Parliaments the names of Eyre and Strachan only appear? A. Yes; I only speak from the information of others respecting Mr. Spottiswoode.
RICHARD PARADISE re-examined. Q. Who gave the name of the firm? A. One of the clerks, by order of Mr. Mc Donald, the overseer.
HENRY WILKINS. I live with Mr. Chip. We did not send this note.
RICHARD PARADISE. He brought this note on Friday, and had a sheet - he brought it back again on Saturday, and said it was the wrong sheet.
CHARLES EAGLETON. I took up the prisoner when I was sent for.
JURY to EDWARD WYNNE. Q. Suppose Mr. Spottiswoode signs orders, how does he do it? A. Eyre and Strachan - he never signs "Spottiswoode" for Eyre and Strachan.
COURT. Q. Do the clerks sign the same way? A. Yes, always.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300527-207]
1237. JOHN LIVERPOOL was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of May, 4 glazed window sashes, value 2l., the goods of the Mayor and Commonalty of the City of London , being fixed to a certain building of theirs; against the Statute.
THREE OTHER COUNTS, varying the manner of stating the property.
MESSRS. BARNABY and BULLOCK conducted the prosecution.
MR. WILLIAM MONTAGUE. I am clerk of the City works. The house, No. 7, Eastcheap, has been purchased to form an approach to the new bridge; I gave orders to the auctioneer to sell it under the Act of Parliament.
J. A. CRIGHTON. I am an auctioner. I was to sell the materials of this house; they were sold by my uncle, and I attended as clerk - the materials were sold to various persons; some sashes and floors were sold to Mr. Brooks, and he sold them to Mr. Shearman.
JAMES SHEARMAN. I bought these sashes of Mr. Brooks two or three days previous to their being stolen; I saw them again on the morning of the 20th of May, inthe frame of the building - I told my man to take them down; I afterwards saw this sash, which is one I bought - I had not authorized any one to bring the sash away; I took the prisoner in consequence of information.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. Have you purchased many houses? A. No; several lots of different articles - I bought some of Mr. Brooks on the Monday previous to the 20th, which was Thursday; the sash was then fixed in the building - there were perhaps twenty sashes in the building, but this was a particular sash; there were no others like this in the building - they were two inch sashes, which is an uncommon size; I do not know how the prisoner became possessed of them - they were on the second floor of the house No. 7, Eastcheap.
CHARLES MEAD. On Thursday, the 20th of May, I was at No. 7, Eastcheap, working at the house, and saw the prisoner, whom I knew before - he was on the bottom floor; I saw one pair of sashes standing against my master's stove - I asked the prisoner whose they were; he said his - I said he must take them away, and he did.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe he is a labourer? A. Yes; I saw him by the sashes - I do not know whose they were.
WILLIAM HAVERSON. I live at Bethnal-green. The prisoner was employed in taking down these premises - on the 20th May I saw him there, about two o'clock, with two sashes on his shoulder; he appeared to me to move quieter than usual - I thought all was not right; the sashes were like this, but I cannot say this was one - this is one I picked out of a hundred at Mr. Shearman's, and which had been on the building.
Cross-examined. Q. When did you pick it out? A. Last Friday week, by Mr. Shearman's request, but whether the prisoner brought this one out, I cannot say; the two he had, had been on the building.
COURT. Q. Are you sure it is one of the four that were on the building? A. Yes, but I cannot be certain it is one the prisoner brought out.
CHARLES FORSTER. I am a labourer, and worked at Eastcheap. I saw the prisoner on Wednesday, but I cannot say at what house; I was talking with him before breakfast, and he lent me a knife - he was taking out two sashes; he was a labourer there.
THOMAS CLOSS. I am a labourer. I saw the prisoner on the premises on the 20th of May, with a pair of sashes - I did not see what he did with them; I did not see him take them out - I have never said I saw him remove them.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300527-208]
1237. THOMAS BEATON was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of May, 1 handkerchief, value 3s. 6d., the goods of Joseph Harvey, from his person.
JOSEPH HARVEY. I lost a handkerchief on the 3rd of May from my left-hand coat pocket in Long-lane - I did not feel it taken, but I heard a noise, looked round, and saw the prisoner in custody; this is the handkerchief - it has my initials on it; I had used it when I was in Chiswell-street, about a quarter of a mile from where I lost it.
THOMAS HOBBS. I saw the prisoner take the handkerchief from the prosecutor's pocket - he had it in his hand, and was putting it into his left-hand pocket; the moment I took him he dropped it.
Prisoner's Defence. There was a man selling stuff to cure corns and worts; a young man there stood talking to me, and the gentleman went by with his handkerchief hanging out of his pocket - I took it to give him, and this gentleman took me.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 13. - Whipped and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18300527-209]
1238. GEORGE PAYNE was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of May, 7 bound books, value 1l. 2s. 6d., the goods of Richard Hughes.
JOHN GRIFFITHS. I am in the employ of Mr. Richard Hughes, a stationer, of Gilbert-street, in the City. On the 22nd of May I saw the prisoner take seven bound books from his shelves - he came first and moved them, then came a second time, and took them away; I ran after him, but could not overtake him - I returned to take care of the shop; he was brought back in less than ten minutes - I am sure he is the boy - I saw him twice that day; I had not seen him before; he dropped the books on the shop floor, as I came down stairs - he did not see me: he had carried them about two yards.
JURY. Q. What distance were you from him? A. Up stairs, about seven yards off.
Prisoner. He said I went three times, and I went in but once. Witness. No, I did not; he had passed the door three times - he then came in, moved the books, and then took them.
ANTHONY REPTON. I am an officer. I took up the prisoner in Hartshorn-court, about six hundred yards from the prosecutor's - he saw some persons at the bottom of the court, and turned back; a butcher caught hold of him - I came up, and took him; he was about two hundred yards from the prosecutor's when I first saw him; the witness was pursuing him, crying Stop thief! and he was taken in five minutes.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 16. - Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18300527-210]
1239. JOHN DEWERT and HENRY NEWMAN were indicted for stealing, on the 25th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 4d., the goods of Isaac Thomas Absell, from his person.
ISAAC THOMAS ABSELL. On the 25th of April my handkerchief was in my left hand pocket - I was in Cow-lane about ten minutes before ten o'clock in the evening; I did not see it taken, but a witness tapped me on the shoulder, and asked if I had lost it; I had had it about a quarter of an hour before - this is it; it has W. P. 2 on it - I did not see the prisoners till I turned round, and a gentleman laid hold of Dewert, and told me to take Newman, which I did - there was a mob, and I was close to them; there was a noise with the watchman or something, I cannot tell what.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. Is it not a very narrow passage? A. No; I looked at the mob, but did not stop a minute - I had not observed any persons behind me till the gentleman touched my shoulder.
THOMAS TOOLE. I was in Cow-lane - I saw the prosecutor there; I saw the two prisoners trying several genmen's pockets - I watched them; they were together following two gentlemen down to Paternoster-row; the prosecutor was stopping, and they came behind him - Dewert took the handkerchief and gave it to the other; they went away together - I laid hold of Dewert, and told the gentleman to take Newman - Dewert said, "What! what!" I said, "You have taken this gentleman's handkerchief;" he said, "I have not got it, search me;" I said, "That is of no use, you have parted with it."
COURT. Q. Where was the prosecutor? A. With his face towards the crowd; there were some persons behind him; the prisoners were two of them.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. How far were you from the prisoners? A. They stood close to the gentleman, and I stood close to them - I did not take them immediately; there were many persons - I might have been knocked down; I do not suppose they observed me - they moved away; I took one, the other went a few doors he then stopped, and turned round; I swear they are the persons I saw behind the prosecutor - I did not see the handkerchief in the prisoners' possession, till the prosecutor said he had got it; there were eight or ten persons, but they did not watch them as I did.
GEORGE BUSH. I was coming down Hosier-lane, and saw the prosecutor hold Newman - I did not see the other till a little while afterwards, when Toole brought him out of King-street; the prisoners were charged with taking the handkerchief out of the pocket - they did not say any thing; when I took hold of Newman the handkerchief dropped from him - I took it to the watch-house.
Cross-examined. Q. Where did you see him? A. In Hosier-lane, ten or twelve yards from Cow-lane - I had only just come out of the watch-house, and was going on duty; I collared Newman, as he was against the shutters; the handkerchief dropped behind him - no persons were behind him.
WILLIAM TURNBULL. I am an officer. I received the prisoners in custody at the watch-house; this is the handkerchief.
The prisoners received a good character.
DEWERT - GUILTY. Aged 17.
NEWMAN - GUILTY. Aged 17.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300527-211]
1240. JOHN CARRINGTON was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of May, 1 canvas bag, value 2d.; 7 sovereigns, and a 5l. Bank note, the property of Thomas Tomlinson, from his person.
THOMAS TOMLINSON. I deal in potatoes and coals, and live in St. John's-lane, Clerkenwell. On the 10th of May, I lost this money out of my left-hand pocket, as I was in Newgate-market, about seven o'clock in the morning - there was a 5l. note, and 7l. in gold, in a canvas bag; I have never got it again; I know the prisoner, and saw him that morning two or three times near me - I had seen him near me about three minutes before I missed my money.
Prisoner. Q. When I was brought before you, did you not say you did not know me? A. I had seen you, but I did not know who took it.
ELIJAH WHITMORE. On the 10th of May I was in the market, filling up a half sieve of gooseberries - I looked up, and saw the prisoner's right hand in Mr. Tomlinson's pocket - he pulled out a very dirty bag; I went to tell Mr. Tomlinson, but my master said, "Come home, I want you;" the prosecutor afterwards came and said he had been robbed - I said I knew the person; the prisoner had got out of the market before the prosecutor said that to my master, but he was taken in the afternoon.
Prisoner. Q. Did not you say your master told you? A. Yes, he said Mr. Tomlinson had been robbed, and I said I knew the person who robbed him; I knew him before, and knew his name.
THOMAS HANDLEY. I am a headborough. The prosecutor came to me on the 10th of May, and said he had been robbed in Newgate-market; I went to look for the prisoner, by the description he gave, to Battle-bridge, where his uncle lives - I met him in the afternoon going up St. John-street, and charged him with it; he said he had not been in the market all that morning, but when I took him to the station he said he had been at the market, but not till eight o'clock - he had only 4s. 6d. on him, but he had a new suit of black on.
Prisoner. I know nothing at all of it - I was out at work that day.
JURY to ELIJAH WHITMORE. Q. How soon after you saw this did you tell of it? A. Directly he told my master - not above two or three minutes after.
THOMAS TOMLINSON. I went across the market, and had occasion to pay some money - I then missed my bag; I went back to the stall I had been at, and then round the market - it was a dirty bag; I am in the coal business.
GUILTY. Aged 25. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18300527-212]
1241. WILLIAM WEBBER was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of April, 1 shirt, value 9s., the goods of Richard Cooper.
RICHARD COOPER. I am a tailor. On the 20th of April I had left my shirt on a box at my lodging when I went out at seven o'clock in the morning; the prisoner lodged in the same house - when he came home at half-past twelve he told me he had pawned it, and said it must be a d-d good one, as he had got 4s. on it; I said I should get an officer, but, in consequence of some of his friends coming the next morning, I let him go.
Cross-examined by Mr. LEE. Q. Do not you know he had been out drinking the whole of that day? A. I do not know - he came to me while I was in bed and said he had pawned it; I had told him before this not to take any of my things - he did not say he took it by mistake; he said he would get it out - 4s. was put down, but I never accepted it; he was taken in bed on the 21st of April - I said if he got it out I did not care.
STEPHEN STEPHENS. This shirt was pawned with me by the prisoner.
FRANCIS GITTENS. I am an officer. I found this duplicate of the shirt on the prisoner.
Witness for the Defence.
MRS. RIGBY. I am landlady of the house; the prisoner was very much intoxicated from the Monday - he came home that Tuesday very drunk; he went up stairs for half an hour, then I saw him no more till he came home at night - he went to the prosecutor, and said he had taken a wrong shirt.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300527-213]
1242. WILLIAM WADHAM and JOHN NOONHAM were indicted for stealing, on the 4th of May, 1 handkerchief, value 4s., the goods of David Maclucas, from his person.
DAVID MACLUCAS. On the 4th of May I was going from Cloth-fair to the City, and felt some person at my pocket - I turned, and saw my handkerchief in the prisoner Noonham's hand; I did not see Wadham at all.(Property produced and sworn to.)
THOMAS CROWDER. I was passing through Cloth-fair, and saw the two prisoners - they had been following another gentleman before they committed this crime; I then saw Noonham go and take the handkerchief from the prosecutor, and Wadham was crossing the road.
JOHN WILLIAM HARRISON. I took the prisoners, and have the property.
MR. MACLUCAS. I did not see Crowder - a Mr. Burke took Noonham with the handkerchief in his hand; he was running away.
Noonham's Defence. It was picked off the ground.
THOMAS CROWDER. I told the officer that the prisoners were together, and Noonham took the handkerchief.
NOONHAM - GUILTY. Aged 13.
Fined One Shilling and Discharged.
WADHAM - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300527-214]
1243. JOHN ROSE and PHILIP PORTER were indicted for stealing, on the 21st of May, 1 handkerchief, value 3s., the goods of James Willson, from his person.
JAMES WILLSON. I lost my handkerchief on the 21st of May - I was told by Webb that it was taken as I was passing through the Bank-chambers about four o'clock- I did not see the prisoner at all; it was safe about an hour before.(Property produced and sworn to.)
HENRY WEBB. I am clerk to a stock-broker. I saw Porter take the handkerchief from the prosecutor's pocket and pass it to Rose, who put it into his pocket - I told the prosecutor; Porter passed the prosecutor - Rose passed me, and was taken by the officer; I went on, and saw Porter tying up his garter - I did not take him, as there were several boys about calling Bill.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. What time was this? A. Near four o'clock in the Bank-chambers - there were only the prisoners, the prosecutor, and myself; I had never seen Porter before - I had plenty of opportunity of looking at him; he was taken the next day I believe - I suppose five or ten minutes elapsed from the time of his taking it, till I saw him again; he was then in a turning leading to Coleman-street - he walked away from the Bank-chambers; I did not think myself bound to take him - I swear distinctly he is the boy that took it.
GEORGE WILLIAM MORGAN. I am an officer. I saw both the prisoners go down Bartholomew-lane, and cross into Bank-chambers - they followed the prosecutor; Rose came running back just as I got to the gate, and I took him - I do not know that I had seen him before that day, but I knew Porter; I am sure they were together - there were two others who, I suppose, were with them, but they did not go in; when they saw the bustle they ran off. I found the handkerchief on Rose.
Porter's Defence. I was taking a walk, and this officer took hold of me, and said, "You will soon see your companion, come along with me."
ROSE - GUILTY. Aged 16.
PORTER - GUILTY. Aged 13.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18300527-215]
1244. JOHN WEST was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of April, 1 pocket-book, value 6d.; 1 bill of exchange, for payment of 200l.; 3 other bills of exchange, for payment of 40l.; 1 other bill of exchange, for payment of 26l., and 1 other bill of exchange, for payment of 34l. 7s. 6d., the property of George Ross.
GEORGE ROSS. I live in Finsbury-square. On the 22nd of April I had a pocket-book in my private coat pocket, containing these different bills of exchange - about twelve o'clock in the day I was in Long-lane; I felt a pull at my coat, put my hand, and missed the book - I then saw the prisoner running across the road, with the pocketbook in his hand; I ran after him - he endeavoured to escape under the head of a horse; he got across, and a gentleman stopped him - he threw down the book, and the papers fell out; I picked them up.
Prisoner. He said there was another boy that run away. Witness. I did not see any other - I saw him run with it in his hand.
HENRY CARTER. I saw the prisoner running under the horses' neck, and the prosecutor after him; I had seen him take the book from the prosecutor - I did not see any other boy; I saw him run with it in his hand, and stopped him.
-. I am an officer. I took the prisoner, and have the book.
The prisoner put in a written Defence, stating that he had found the pocket-book under a cart in Smithfield. He received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 14.
Recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor and Jury.
Confined Three Months and Whipped.
[trialAccount t18300527-216]
1245. JOHN MOORE was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of May, 2 3/4 lbs. of soap, value 18d., and 3 lbs. weight of flour, value 6d., the goods of William Wilson and others, his masters.
WILLIAM WILSON. I am in partner ship with two others. The prisoner was in our employ for five years, at our warehouse in Martin's-lane; he was entrusted with the keys to open the gates in the morning. In consequence of information I stopped in the warehouse on Thursday night last, with a witness; I heard the prisoner go into a room and take this property - I saw him before he left the room; the witness was in that room, and I was in the floor above - when I heard him going out of the room I went down, and found him in custody.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. Was it not his business to go into that part of the premises? A. No.(Property produced and sworn to.)
JOHN PING. I was in the room, and at half-past five o'clock the prisoner opened the outer gates and came in -I saw him through the window; he came into the room by some means, and searched the pockets of the prosecutor's coat, which hung there - he then spread his handkerchief out, and took out three cakes of soap, and put one of them into his handkerchief; he then went to a cupboard, and took some unfinished hats, but he put themback again - he then took this flour, and was going to leave the room: I seized him in the door-way, and found these articles on him.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you ever quarrelled with him? A. No; I told him I thought he did not do his duty by not lighting the fires, and coming in good time - I never said I would do for him; I should be sorry to use such an unmanly expression.
JOHN FREE. I am an officer, and took the prisoner. -These things were given me by the prosecutor, who said he had got them by means of this key; the prisoner said nothing.
Prisoner's Defence. The witness has told a great many lies, to say he never quarrelled with me.
The prisoner received an excellent character.
GUILTY. Aged 45.
Recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutors.
Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18300527-217]
1246. SARAH JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of May, 2 half-crowns, 8 shillings, and 4 sixpences, the monies of John Bell, from his person.
JOHN BELL. I am a lighterman. I met the prisoner last Thursday in the street; I went to the public-house to give her something to drink, and then walked by the side of the bridge - I had 16s. or 17s. in my pocket; just as I was leaving her I put my hand into my pocket, and my money was gone: I had not a farthing - I accused her of taking my money; she said she had not, that what money she had was her own - the night-constable was on the spot, and he took her; when she got to the watch-house she threw some money on the table, and said it was all the money she had - the officer went to where we had been, to see if he could find any there; he could not - he then came back; the prisoner stooped down, and threw the rest of the silver on the floor - there were 15s. found in all; I lost about 17s. - I had it safe at the public-house when I paid for some liquor.
Prisoner. He took me and two other women, and treated us - he was not sober. Witness. I was not quite sober; there was no other woman with us.
GEORGE BAYLIS. About one o'clock on Tuesday morning I saw the prosecutor and prisoner cavelling together -I went up; he said she had robbed him of 15s. - I took her to the watch-house: she threw 4s. 6d. on the table, and declared it was all she had - the night officer searched her in my presence, and the rest of the money dropped from some part of her garments; the whole money we found was 15s., which was what the prosecutor stated - he was intoxicated, but not very bad.
JAMES PEACHEY. I am a constable. I searched the prisoner, and found the money in the bottom of her petticoat; she put her hand down, and threw it on the floor - she had at first thrown down the 4s. 6d., and said it was all she had in the world.
Prisoner's Defence. I produced my pocket, and said I could give account of the money I had; I had changed a sovereign in the morning, and paid part of it away - if I had had more time I could have sent for the gentleman who gave it me.
JAMES PEACHEY. She was intoxicated - she did not say she had changed a sovereign; she said positively at first that she had but 4s. 6d.
GUILTY. Aged 32. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18300527-218]
1247. WILLIAM WELLS was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of May, 1 pair of shoes, value 2s. 6d., the goods of Jacob Russell.
THOMAS EMBERSON. I live with Mr. Jacob Russell, a pawnbroker. On the 28th of May a pair of shoes were taken from about half a foot within the door; a person might get them by getting on the step - there are two steps; I heard the noise, went round, and saw the prisoner with them; I took him directly.
Prisoner. The shoes were given to me by a boy. Witness. There was no other boy.
ROBERT HOWARD. I have a pair of woman's shoes, and took the prisoner - he said he took a knife and cut them down; he said he was very hungry at the time.
GUILTY. Aged 12. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300527-219]
1248. JOHN TABBY was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of April, 4 half-crowns, the monies of Joseph George, from his person.
JOSEPH GEORGE. I lost four half-crowns on the 24th of April from my left-hand breeches pocket, between twelve and one o'clock in the morning, as I was passing Smithfield-bars - I took out half a crown to go and have some supper, and my money fell down; I picked it up, and was walking away when the prisoner came and put his hand into my pocket - I caught his hand, but he got from me; the officer took him - I cannot be positive of his person - I was very much in liquor.
JOHN SNOWSILL. I was in Smithfield - I saw the prosecutor take out half a crown, which he dropped; he stooped to pick it up. and some one shoved him over it -I turned, and seized a tall man; he said he was not the man - the prosecutor then said he was robbed of four half-crowns.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. He was very tipsy? A. Yes - he said he was robbed, and several persons came up; I did not see the prisoner till the officer had hold of him.
JOHN CARLISLE. I am an officer. I was present and saw the mob - I went up, and heard the prosecutor say he had dropped half a crown, and on stooping to pick it up, some person shoved him down; I stood close at his elbow, and all in a minute the prisoner came and put his hand into his left-hand pocket, and put something into his own pocket; I saw the prosecutor lay hold of his wrist, but he twisted from him - I followed, and took him; I took hold of one of his hands, and told the watchman to take his other hand; I took him to the Compter, and found four half-crowns in his left-hand pocket.
Cross-examined. Q. What did the prosecutor say? A. He said he dropped one, and had four more in his pocket - I gave this account to the Magistrate; when he had taken the money he ran into the mob, but was not out of my sight a minute - when I took him, the prosecutor said, "He is the man that had his hand in my pocket;" he did express some doubt of him, but he was drunk.
MR. CLARKSON to JOSEPH GEORGE. Q. Is it true that you cannot say whether the prisoner is the person? A. I thought he was at the time, but I cannot be sure, beingin such a state of intoxication; I did not point out any one else - I pointed to the prisoner, and said, "That is the person;" the officer came up directly he heard of it - I did not say I was robbed till he had put his hand into my pocket; I do not know whether the officer was on the spot when his hand was in my pocket.
MR. CLARKSON to JOHN SNOWSILL. Q. Was the officer there at the time the prosecutor said he was robbed? A. I cannot tell; I do not think he was so near as to see a person commit the robbery - I was holding a tall man, and was accusing him of picking up the half-crown; there were a great many people there - I did not see the officer; I cannot tell whether I was near enough to have seen him.
Prisoner's Defence. I had been taking supper at an eating-house; when I came out I saw the prosecutor - he said he had been robbed, and charged several people with the theft; I said I had half a crown in my pocket, and may be that was his, and the officer took me - I had received four half-crowns and two shillings.
GUILTY. Aged 23. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18300527-220]
1249. JOHN DEAN and WILLIAM DEACON were indicted for stealing, on the 5th of May, 1 handkerchief, value 5s., the goods of William Corbett, from his person.
BENJAMIN CHARLES MURRAY. I saw Dean take a handkerchief from the prosecutor's pocket, near William-street, Blackfriars, on the 5th of May, between eight and nine o'clock at night; he gave it to Deacon, who put it into his pocket - I told the prosecutor, then followed them over the bridge till I met an officer, and gave them in charge; I saw Deacon throw down the handkerchief as he was going back - a person took it up, and delivered it at the station-house; the prosecutor was not there that night, but he owned it the next day.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. Was it dark? A. It was dusky; I was on one side of the bridge and they on the other; I could distinctly see Dean take it - the handkerchief was light, and the gentleman was dressed in black.
Deacon. I am sure he could not see us; he has been a witness here a dozen times. Witness. I have been a street-keeper, and have had a number of cases here.
COURT. Q. Did you keep your eyes upon them? A. Yes; they were not out of my sight, except when I turned my head to speak to the prosecutor.
MR. LEE. Q. Were you not in the old Police? A. No. I was in the new Police, but have been discharged three weeks for being asleep on my beat - it was not for being drunk.
WALTER FLEMING. I am a Police-man. I was on duty on the 5th instant; Murray pointed out the prisoners to me, and with his assistance I took them both - as we were going to the station-house, I told Murray to walk behind to see that they did not put their hands to their pockets - and in Pell-street, Deacon was in the act of pulling out this handkerchief, and this other with it - he threw them down; one of the men took them up, and took them to the station-house.
Cross-examined. Q. What time elapsed between Murray coming to you, and your taking them? A. Not more than five minutes; he must have lost sight of them; I dare say they did not expect to be taken - I dare not search a man till I take him to the inspector.
WILLIAM CORBETT. This is my handkerchief, which I had taken from me on the 5th of May - it has no mark on it, but I believe it is mine; I did not see either of the prisoners till they were in custody.
Cross-examined. Q. Is it not a very common pattern? A. I do not know Sir - I believe it to be mine.
DEAN - GUILTY. Aged 18.
DEACON - GUILTY. Aged 18.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18300527-221]
1250. EDWARD ARSLETT was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of May, 4 yards of ribbon, value 2s. 6d., the goods of John Wade and another, his masters.
JOHN WADE. I am a ribbon-dresser, in partnership with my father, we live in Flying Horse-court, Maiden-lane - the prisoner was in my employ for ten months. On the 12th of May I had some ribbons from Flower and Moss to be watered; they were measured in my presence, and were eighteen yards each - I had had complaints of their goods being short before; I saw these watered on the 13th of May, and gave them to the prisoner to spindle up, and left the work-shop to give him an opportunity of cutting them - I went in, and asked how they looked; I then saw one was cut - I measured them, and found each of the four half pieces, one yard short; I called in a friend, and while I was doing that the prisoner ran down stairs- in about a quarter of an hour the odd yards were found in the dust-hole; he was taken, and denied having cut them.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. Had you charged him with cutting them before you called the person? A. No; I had seen these ribbons measured, and marked at each end; they were measured by being turned on a reel- there were eighteen yards each, and are now a yard short; they sometimes are a quarter of a yard or half a yard short - they were in my possession from the 12th to the 13th; I have three persons in my employ; I did not give them to the prisoner till the 13th - I was not absent from him above ten minutes; I had not measured them on the 13th, but I saw the mark at each end, when they passed through the machine; they do not shrink in watering, that I am aware of.
WILLIAM LEE MOORE. I measured these four half pieces of ribbon on the evening of the 12th; three were a little over, and one a little short of the eighteen yards.
Cross-examined. A. Are they not sometimes half a yard shorter or longer? A. Yes, but three of them were over, and one was a trifle short - we marked them at each end.
DANIEL FORRESTER. I was sent for; I found nothing on the prisoner - we went down to the cellar, and found nothing; we went up stairs again; the prosecutor then went down into the cellar by himself, with a piece of iron, and called out,
"Here is something in a paper;" I went, and took up this.
Cross-examined. Q. Were there many persons in the house? A. Yes; I should think it cannot be done without three persons; the premises are three or four stories- no one had been out but a lad for some small beer.
JOHN WADE re-examined. I locked them up on the 12th, and at twelve o'clock, on the 13th, they were taken out and watered; I left them for the prisoner to spindle- I went into the cellar first with the officer; but from my certainly that they must be somewhere I went down again with a candle, and found them; there was a footstep on the dust.
Cross-examined. Q. How long did you leave the prisoner? A. About ten minutes; I went to the window, and he left the shop - I could hear him distinctly go to the bottom of the stairs; he said he went out, but if he had I must have seen him.
Prisoner's Defence. They were given to me to spindle which I did, and left them on the table; it might be as likely for my master to cut them - he has a bad character, so has his father.
JURY to DANIEL FORRESTER. Q. Did you search the dust-hole? A. I looked in, and saw some papers and rubbish- this ribbon might have been there; I cannot say whether it had been under the dust - he called me down, and said,
"Here is a paper, I wish you would pick it up," there might be a little dust in the paper; I do not think he was down there two minutes.
JOHN WADE re-examined. I did not put it there - the officer did not search so much as he ought to have done.
JURY. Q. Had you had any complaints before this? A. Yes, and we had detected a person two months before - the prisoner was not with us then; he had the best of characters, and a respectable house was going to take him as a blocker.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300527-222]
1251. JAMES CROUCH and MARY WELCH were indicted for feloniously putting off to Leonard John Zimmerman, 8 counterfeit shillings, at and for a lower rate and value, than the same did by their denomination import, (i.e.)for 2s.
MESSRS. SCARLETT and ELLIS conducted the prosecution.
LEONARD JOHN ZIMMERMAN. I am a gem polisher. On the 6th of May, before ten o'clock in the morning, I went to the house of Seager, who is inspector of the watch in the ward of Aldersgate; I was then searched by Bates, in his presence - they gave me two shillings which I had seen marked; I then went to a place in Little Britain, according to Seager's orders - I had seen Crouch once before in Smithfield; I waited in Little Britain till about half-past three o'clock - Crouch then came to me and asked what I wanted; I said I wanted ten pegs, which is a nick-name they give to counterfeit shillings - he was then by himself; he and I went on together down to the Blue coat school gate - he there went up to Welch and spoke to her; she gave him ten counterfeit shillings in paper from under her frock; I gave him the two shillings which Seager gave me - he gave them to Welch; he and I went into the Blue coat school gate, and when we got sixteen or eighteen paces in he gave me eight shillings -I told him I wanted but eight; I looked at them, and know they are part of what the girl gave him, because he had kept them in his right hand, and I had not lost sight of that hand - she told Crouch she was going to see her mother, and when he gave me the eight shillings he said the other two were for somebody else; in a few minutes the officer came and took him - I gave him the eight shillings.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. How do you get your living? A. By work - I worked for Mr. Seagar's brother, who is a gun-maker, for six years; I do not get my living by entrapping people for the Mint, but by honest industry, the whole of the last twelve months as a gun-maker and doing odd jobs; I never was engaged in any thing of this kind before - I do not know a person of the name of Bill, and never was employed but on this occasion; I swear I only heard the term pegs a fortnight before - Mr. Seager told me the slang names they gave them; Mr. Seager knew me by working for his brother; I do not expect to get any thing for this - I have had no promise held out to me; I do not know that I am to get any thing - I came here to serve Mr. Seager; I had seen Crouch in Little Britain before this, and I had seen him in Smithfield on the 4th, when I went to look after a man named Nash - not exactly on this business; I was employed by Seagar to detect him - I was never employed to entrap any but the prisoner and Nash; I was looking for Nash and the prisoner spoke to me - I had never seen him before; he came and asked who was I looking for, was I looking for Nash? I said I had rather see Winter-bank; I had never seen Winterbank; but I had heard of his name - I did not go after Winterbank, he was in the Compter; Mr. Seager had told me so, but I wanted the prisoner to think I was in the line; I said it as a blind -I understand Welch is about eleven years of age; the officers were about eight yards from me when I met Crouch; I was on one side of the way and they on the other, up a court - he might see them; I have not been drilled in this business - I was close to him when the little girl gave him the money open; it was not in paper; I did not say a word about paper - we went into the Bluecoat school yard and eight shillings were given to me; he had the ten shillings in his right hand - I watched his hand to see whether he put the two shillings away, as I wanted but eight; I did not then see Bates or Seager -I took it for granted they were not in the passage then; the Blue coat School-yard is a large open yard - when I took the money Bates came and took Crouch, and Seager took me; the two shillings I had given Crouch he gave to Welch - I had not been more than eight or ten minutes with him before he was taken; no one was by to hear any conversation between us.
MR. ELLIS. Q. Had the officer any particular dress? A. No - Welch took the coin loose and gave it loose to Crouch; When Crouch came and introduced himself to me on the 4th, he and I walked down Hosier-lane - he said Tom was in the Compter and he had got the things - I said I did not know that it was right with him; he said if I thought it was not, I might go to the Compter, and ask Tom about it - he asked what I wanted - I said two tushes, meaning half-crowns; he wanted me to give him two shillings, but I would not - he then went in, and brought two half-crowns, wrapped in a piece of paper, and I gave him a shilling for it; I did that under the direction of Mr. Seager - I have worked for Mr. Wilk's, in Church-lane, at repairing guns.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. How much haveyou got by jobs of this sort? A. I never in my life received a farthing, or any thing else, nor do I expect it; on the 5th of May I went to the Fortune of War, at the corner of Cock-lane, with Crouch - we had a pint of porter, and I paid for it.
COURT. Q. Where did you get the money? A. I had it in my pocket; it has not been repaid.
SAMUEL SEAGER. I am superintendent of the ward of Aldersgate. I know Zimmerman - he is a gun-polisher, and works generally for the trade; he is a hard working, industrious, sober man; I applied to him to assist me in detecting the prisoners, or any other person selling counterfeit coin; he acted under my direction on the 4th and 5th of May - on the 6th he came to my house, about nine o'clock in the morning; Bates searched him in my presence - I marked two shillings and gave them to him; I marked them so that I should know them again, and told him to go to Little Britain, and by no means to go out of my sight, but wait till Cronch came, who had appointed to meet him there that morning - Bates was in company with me - he waited at a public-house till three o'clock in the afternoon; we were waiting at a public-house opposite, till Cronch joined him - they then went on towards the Blue coat school; I followed, in company with Bates, till we came to Montague-court, and there I turned up and waited a few minutes - I saw Crouch set down by the side of Welch, on the coping which supports the iron railing - the witness was standing; Crouch got up in a minute or two; I saw him speak to Welch, who put her hand under her frock, and gave him something; I do not know what - I then saw the witness give Cronch something, which he transferred to Welch; they then went into the Blue coat school-yard - Welch remained outside; I then crossed from Montague-court, and looked through the iron railing; in the mean time I saw Crouch give something to the witness - Bates seized Crouch, and the witness proceeded on at a quick pace, but I went and took him; he gave me these eight shillings, much in the same state they are now in - they were not in paper; I marked them - Zimmerman went about his business; I saw Bates scuffling with the prisoner in the yard - I saw Bates pick up one shilling, and a blue coat boy picked up another; I then took the prisoner to the Compter -Bates searched him, and I searched him afterwards; nothing was found on him - I said,
"You thought you were very cunning, did you not?" he said," I get my living in an honest way; I serve the hospital" - I believe that was all that passed; not finding any thing on Crouch, and knowing that the money had passed from Zimmerman, we thought Welch had got them - we took her under the archway of Bartholomew-hospital; two women were with her - seeing her so young a girl we thought she might be acting under instructions; we took them all three to a public-house, and upon Welch we found nineteen bad shillings, 7s. 6d. good money, and 5 1/2d. in copper; two of the good ones were those I had marked in the morning; these are them - they are marked in the middle of the letter G.; I also found on her fifteen half-crowns - the money was in a double pocket, under her frock; the good money was on the left side, and the bad on the right - here is the counterfeit money.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you followed this reputable employment? A. I have been superintendent of the ward some time - I have been about four months looking after these kind of persons; the ward employ me- I am not employed by the Mint; this was voluntary on my part - I consider it my duty to prevent crime; I do not encourage persons to go and entrap others - I get nothing for this whatever but my pay; these two shillings were my own money - I have never received any thing, nor do I expect any thing; I solemnly declare on my oath that not one word has passed between me and any officer of the Mint on this subject - I do not know whether the blue coat boy is here; I did not bring him - I left it to those who had the conducting of this business; I have known the witness six or eight years - I met him at my brother's, and told him I wanted him to do this business - he volunteered at once; though I was satisfied with his honesty, I thought it my duty to search and watch him - I kept him in sight all the way, till he came to the Blue coat school-yard.
MR. SCARLETT. Q. I understand for about four months your attention has been drawn to some transactions of this nature? A. Yes - I consider it my duty as an officer to look after these people, and to give information to the officers of the Mint - I am not in any other way employed by them; I am paid for the time I am in attendance by the County or the city; I believe expences of witnesses are usually paid.
THOMAS BATES. I am superintendent of the ward of Aldersgate; I have heard Seager's evidence - it is perfectly true: while I was scuffling with Crouch he was trying to shake something from his right-hand, which was 2s.; I seized him with one arm - I was picking a shilling up when Seager advanced; I thought one had rolled down a grating, but a blue coat boy named Burns gave it to me - these are them; they have not been out of my possession since I seized them in his hand; as soon as I took one up I saw it was bad - I took Welch, and found on her those two shillings, which I had marked before.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you been in this employ? A. No time at all, but it is my duty as an officer; we have so many complaints in the ward of Aldersgate - every shopkeeper is burthened with bad coin; I took George Fowler on the 19th of April - it was my own voluntary act; I expect nothing for it - it has cost me money out of my own pocket; the coin was not in paper - I believe it was open in her hand; I was nearest the witness, when he gave his evidence, and did not hear the word"paper" - there was not time to fold them in paper; I made application for the Blue coat boy to come, but it is not allowed unless an application is made from a Magistrate.
MR. JOHN FIELD. These shillings are all counterfeit, the eight and the two - they are cast in a mould, and are the same metal; good shillings are milled.
Crouch's Defence. I never did any thing of the kind; I never got my living that way - I never was in confinement in my life.
CROUCH - GUILTY. Aged 40.
Transported for Seven years.
WELSH - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300527-223]
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
1252. GEORGE FOWLER was indicted for a misdemeanor. NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300527-224]
1253. SUSANNAH HOWE was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. ELLIS conducted the prosecution.
CHARLES THOROGOOD. I am a constable, and live in Somerset-street, Whitechapel. On Saturday, the 1st of May, at half-past twelve o'clock, I was at the back of the Royal Exchange, and saw the prisoner and a man close together in company, come from under the Piazzas; when they parted, the man went into a biscuit-shop, and the prisoner went round the corner; I told my brother officer that something was not right, and told him to watch the prisoner; I turned my back, and saw the man in the shop- I saw the shopwoman in the shop, take up something off the counter, and go into the parlour with it: I took the man, but not the prisoner.
GEORGE WILLIAM MORGAN. I am a constable, and was with Thorogood; I watched the prisoner, who was at the corner of Sweeting's-rents - she came back to the corner, as if to look towards the biscuit-shop; (the man was discharged by the Magistrate;) when she crossed the road I said, "I want you," and I saw her put something into her left hand - I said,
"You have something in your hand;" she said,
"No, I have not;" I took hold of her hand, and took her into a house - I found this bit of rag in her hand; I asked what it contained - she said,
"Money - good money to be sure;" I asked whether it was shillings or sovereigns - she said she did not know, she had found it; it contained these five shillings.
MR. JOHN FIELD. These five shillings are all counterfeit; they are cast, and appear to be from the same mould.
Prisoner's Defence. I saw a man run by the Royal Exchange - I then saw these, and took them up; I had not had them two minutes.
JURY to CHARLES THOROGOOD. Q. Did the man who went into the shop offer any bad money? A. Yes, this bad shilling.
COURT. Q. And they were in company together? A. Yes, and that induced us to watch them.
GUILTY. - Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18300527-225]
1254. MARGARET TAYLOR was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. ELLIS conducted the prosecution.
MARY STEPTO. I keep a butcher's-shop in Farringdon-market. The prisoner purchased a bit of meat of me on Saturday, the 10th of April, between four and five o'clock in the afternoon; it came to 8d. or 9d. - she offered a very bad shilling; I stopped her, and would not let her have the shilling back - Hopkins looked at it, said it was very bad, and gave it to the officer - I am sure the prisoner is the woman: she was taken to be examined.
WILLIAM HOPKINS. I am a butcher, and live next door to Stepto. I received a shilling from her on the 10th of April - she asked if it was a good one; it was a very bad one - I asked the prisoner if she had any more; she made some reply I could not make out - I sent for Johnson, the officer, and gave him the same shilling.
ROBERT JOHNSON. I am a watchman. I received the shilling from Hopkins on the 10th of April - I marked it, and gave it to Lloyd about half an hour afterwards; I took the prisoner down to the clerk's office - she was searched by a female; nothing was found on her - in going from the office she said she did not care a d-n, for they had only found a shilling about her, and could not do any thing with her - Mrs. Lloyd asked her if she knew Mr. Powell, the solicitor of the Mint; she said, Yes, perfectly well, but he could not hurt her - she was discharged; she gave herself the name of Margaret Taylor.
JAMES LLOYD. I am a constable. This is the shilling I got from the witness - I am certain it is the same; I told the prisoner she had been up to the hall many times, and asked her if she knew Mr. Powell; she said Yes, but she did not care a d-n about Mr. Powell or me either.
SUSANNAH STAPLES. I am a widow, and keep the Queen's Arms, St. Martin's-le-grand. On the 19th of April, about six o'clock in the afternoon, the prisoner came to the bar - she had a glass of gin, and brought this bad half-crown - I gave her 2s. 4d. in change, in two shillings and 4d. in copper; the gin came to 2d.; I put the half-crown into the till among some halfpence - I am sure I had not another half-crown there - Mr. Purser came, and asked what she had given me; I took out the half-crown and gave him, which he saw was bad - the officer had the half-crown; I am certain the prisoner is the person.
WILLIAM PURSER. I have heard Staples' evidence; it is correct - I knew the prisoner's voice, and ran to see where she was; I saw her in the bar, taking up the change - I asked what she had given, and saw this half-crown.
ROBERT TYRRELL. I am a constable, and was sent for to Staples' - it was said the prisoner had offered this half-crown, which Staples marked and gave me; I found 4d. in halfpence on her, but no silver - she said she did not change the half-crown, but changed a sixpence; this was when Staples said she brought the half-crown - there was another woman with the prisoner when I took her, but there was nothing on her.
MR. FIELD. This shilling and half-crown are bad.
GUILTY. Aged 28. - Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18300527-226]
1255. MARGARET TOMKINS was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. ELLIS conducted the prosecution.
URIAH BRYANT. I am a tin-plate worker, and live in Beech-street, Barbican. The prisoner came to my shop on the 17th of April, to buy a sancepan; I showed her one, which came to 8d. - she gave me half a crown; I looked at it, and thought it was bad - I gave it to my man to go to my next door neighbour; he brought it back in a minute, and said it was bad; I gave it to my son, and sent for an officer, who took the prisoner - I am sure she is the person.
DAVID OWEN. My master gave me the half-crown which I paid to Mr. Whitaker, and then gave it back to my master.
ADAM URIAH BRYANT. I am the prosecutor's son - I received the half-crown, and sent for the officer; I marked it, and gave it to Mr. Ripton - the prisoner went by the name of Tobin.
ANTHONY RIPTON. I took the prisoner, and received this half-crown - I was at her examination; she gave the name of Tobin there, I am sure.
MR. JOHN FIELD. This is a bad one.
JAMES COMFORT. I am a butcher, and live with my father, in Farringdon-market. On the 30th of April the prisoner bought a piece of meat of me at my father's shop, it came to 20d. - she offered me half a crown; I took it to my next door neighbour, who said it was bad -Mr. Harrison took her in charge; she called herself Tomkins.
ANTONIO JULIAN WILLIAM HARRISON. I am clerk of Farringdon-market. The moment this half-crown was rung, I found it bad; I marked it, and sent for the beadle - I gave the half-crown and the prisoner to him.
JAMES LLOYD. I received this half-crown from Harrison; the prisoner was searched, and a good half-crown was found on her; she gave the name of Tomkins.
MR. FIELD. This is also counterfeit - they are both cast from Britannia metal.
GUILTY.
Confined Six Months, and to find Sureties for Six Months longer.
[trialAccount t18300527-227]
1256. ELIZA WHEELER FAYERBROTHER was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of May, 14 yards of silk, value 3l., the goods of Mary Niblett, her mistress.
MARY NIBLETT. I am single; the prisoner was in my employ as a day worker - I live in Dorset-street, Salisbury-square, and am a dress-maker - she lived three weeks or a month with me. I missed this property on Tuesday last - I at first suspected a servant, whom I had parted with, and from her confession I suspected the prisoner; I charged her with it on Friday, and said it would be better to confess.
WILLIAM IVEY. I am a shopman to Mr. Reeves, of Snow-hill. This silk was pawned by the prisoner on the 24th of May - she came again on the Friday, and said she had lost the duplicate, would I let her have it; I said not without an affidavit, and it was then too late to get one.
Prisoner's Defence. I did not intend to keep it - she promised if I would own it. she would not hurt me, and gave me till Saturday to redeem it.
MARY NIBLETT. I did not give her any stated time.
GUILTY. Aged 27.
Recommended to Mercy believing it to be her first offence.
Confined Six Weeks.
[trialAccount t18300527-228]
1257. GEORGE BROOKES & JAMES NICHOLSON were indicted for stealing, on the 24th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 2s. 6d., the goods of Joseph Teversham, from his person.
SECOND COUNT, stating it to be the goods of a person whose name is unknown.
GREGORY WILLIAM CEELY. I am a boot-closer. On the evening of the 24th of April, about a quarter before seven o'clock, I was standing in the shop, No. 33, Ludgate-street, I saw the two prisoners on the opposite side of the way; two gentlemen were passing - Brookes took a handkerchief from one of their pockets and gave it to Nicholson - I told my employer of it; he said I had a right to go and stop them; I went and took Brookes, and said he had been robbing a gentleman; he said he had not - another young man saw Nicholson, and he followed and took him with the handkerchief; the gentleman gave his direction at the office - I do not know him.
JAMES GUNNING. I work with Ceely, and was in the shop; he said he saw two men pick the gentleman's pocket; I had seen the prisoner in company, and saw Brookes take the handkerchief and give it to Nicholson; I ran and took Nicholson in Pilgrim-street; I took this handkerchief from him - the gentleman proved it to be his, and I believe it was; I never saw the gentleman before, but I heard his name was Teversham.
GEORGE WEBB. I am an officer. I took Brookes and my brother officer the other; this is the handkerchief -Mr. Teversham saw it at Guildhall - he is not here.
Brookes Defence. I am certain I never picked the pocket - I was going to work.
Nicholson. I hope you will show a little mercy - it is the first time.
BROOKES - GUILTY. Aged 45.
NICHOLSON - GUILTY. Aged 25.
Transported for Seven Years.
